```mermaid
    ID -- materialIndex --> MD["MaterialData[]<br/>Material, MaterialId"]
    MD -- texture indices --> T["gTextures[] (set 0, binding 0)<br/>bindless Sampler2D array;<br/>gCubeTextures[] on the same binding"]
```

In `vertMain`:
- The draw's index in the list is the SPIR-V `DrawIndex` built-in, read with `spirv_asm` in `core.slang` (`getDrawIndex`), plus `pc.firstDraw`. `DrawIndex` restarts at 0 in each indirect call, so `firstDraw` is the index of the call's first draw.
- `drawInfoPtr[draw].firstInstance` is the instance row (core Vulkan allows a non-zero `firstInstance` only with `drawIndirectFirstInstance`). The matrices come from `instancesPtr[row]`.
- Slang lowers `SV_VertexID` to `VertexIndex - BaseVertex`, so the shader adds the draw's own `vertexOffset` (`drawInfoPtr[draw].vertexOffset`) back before indexing `vertsPtr` (`getVertex`; a posed skinned or morphed sub-mesh's vertex comes from `skinnedVertsPtr` at its `InstanceTransform::skinnedVertex` plus the raw index instead, below). The vertex color comes from the color pool, at the geometry row's `colorOffset` plus the same raw index (`getVertexColor`: white for `colorOffset` -1; RGB decoded from sRGB to linear). It is interpolated in linear space and multiplies the base color, unlit too.
- The instance's `materialIndex` goes to the fragment shader. `fragMain` reads `materialsPtr[materialIndex]`, picks each texture's UV set, and with `MATERIAL_TEXTURE_TRANSFORM` applies the five `KHR_texture_transform`s in one branch, uniform over the draw (a branch per texture costs the opaque draws about 6% in `05_city`). It then branches on `materialId`: `MaterialId.UNLIT` calls `shadeUnlit` in `unlit.slang`, and everything else calls `shadePBR` in `pbr.slang`.
- `shadePBR` adds the scene's environment light, or a small constant without one (§4, image-based lighting), and the scene's lights (`createLight`, `rasm/light.h`; the three loops of §4, lights). A directional light shines along `direction`. A point light falls off with the inverse square of the distance, times a window that reaches 0 at `range` (none when `range` is 0). A spot light (`LightType::SPOT`) is a point light times the glTF `KHR_lights_punctual` cone: `saturate(cos(angle to direction) · spotScale + spotOffset)` squared, full inside `innerConeAngle` and 0 beyond `outerConeAngle`, with `spotScale` and `spotOffset` from the angles in `prepareSceneView` (`setSpotCone`). `createLight` rejects a spot light without 0 ≤ inner < outer ≤ π/2. Each light is then multiplied by its shadow (§4).

### CPU/GPU struct contract

The C++ structs in `src/renderer/uberMaterialTypes.h` and the Slang structs in `shaders/lib/core.slang` (with `geometryTypes.slang` and `animationTypes.slang`, which it includes and which the Animate and Skin passes include alone) must match byte for byte under **scalar layout**. The C++ side `static_assert`s the sizes and key offsets (`SceneData` = 2248 bytes, a multiple of 8 since views sit back to back, `Light` = 68, `ShadowedLight` = 12, `ShadowMap` = 72, `InstanceData` = 160, `InstanceTransform` = 168, `AnimatedEntity` = 120, `AnimationNodeData` = 96, `AnimationMeshData` = 96, `AnimationSubMeshData` = 36, `AnimationJointData` = 52, `AnimationFrame` = 112, `SkinVertex` = 16, `MorphDelta` = 24, `MaterialData` = 312, `GeometryData` = 200 with `MAX_MESH_LODS` = 8, `GeometryLod` = 20, `GeometryMeshlet` = 40, …), and `rasm::Vertex` is asserted equal to `gfx::VertexFull`. Those asserts only pin the C++ side, so `validateRasmStructs` (`shaderCompiler.cpp`) checks each struct's size and member offsets against Slang reflection, in every build: against the `StructLayout`s `collectStructLayouts` took after linking, or the ones a shader cache entry stored with its SPIR-V (§7), so a cached shader is checked like a compiled one and a C++ change is caught without a recompile. A mismatch logs each wrong size and offset and fails the shader, so its pipeline is not created. Read shader layouts by walking from `pc` through the pointers: `ProgramLayout::getTypeLayout` reports std140/std430 padding instead. When you change a struct, change both sides, the asserts and the `validateRasmStructs` tables.

## 5. Materials and textures

- `rasm::Material` (`rasm/material.h`) is a CPU-side record:
  - `textures[PbrSlot::COUNT]` and `texCoords[...]`,
  - a flat `float params[]` indexed by `PbrParam` offsets (albedo is 4 floats at offset 0, its alpha the glTF base color alpha; metallic is at 4, …; `EMISSIVE_STRENGTH` at 11 is `KHR_materials_emissive_strength`, 1 by default, and `convertToGfxMaterial` multiplies it into the GPU emissive factor),
  - `textureTransforms[]`, one `TextureTransform` (`offset`, `rotation` in radians, `scale`: `KHR_texture_transform`) per slot; `METALLIC`'s is the metallic-roughness texture's. `toMaterialData` turns each into the rows of a 2×2 matrix and an offset, `uv' = translation · rotation · scale · uv` (rotation as the glTF sample viewer and three.js read it, checked against `TextureTransformTest`), and sets `MATERIAL_TEXTURE_TRANSFORM` when one is not the identity,
  - `alphaMode` (`AlphaMode::NONE`, glTF's `OPAQUE`, which is a macro in `<windows.h>`; `MASK`; `BLEND`) and `alphaCutoff` (0.5), §4.
- `convertToGfxMaterial` (`gpuScene.cpp`) turns it into the GPU `gfx::Material`, which `writeMaterialRow` puts in the material table (§4). `updateMaterial(handle, values)` replaces a material's values at runtime: it gives new textures bindless slots and rewrites the row. Entities that use a texture still loading stop drawing until it finishes (§4). Texture handles are replaced by bindless slot indices, and `INVALID_TEXTURE_INDEX` (0xFFFFFFFF) means "no texture".
- **Bindless slots.** `Renderer::addBindlessTexture` → `RenderContext::updateBindlessDescriptorSet` writes the texture into a slot and stores it in `TextureTable::slots` (`getBindlessTextureIndex` reads it, no hash). A texture holds one slot: asked for any slot, it returns its own unchanged (a frame in flight may sample it; another sampler than `slotSamplers` holds logs an error), else takes one from `freeBindlessSlots`, else `nextBindlessTextureIndex++`. A named slot is taken from the texture that held it (`slotTextures` names each slot's texture), the counter moves past it, and the slots skipped go on the free list; a slot that waits in the release queue (`slotWaiting`) is refused. Destroying a texture gives its slot back through the release queue (§2), so it is reused only after every frame that may sample it (`B25`). `Renderer::destroyTexture` first removes the texture from every material and rewrites their rows. A render target rebuilt with the device idle takes its old textures' slots (`takeBindlessSlot`) and writes the new textures into them at once. When every slot is held or waits, the error names how many wait.
- **Texture upload** (`P2`; the guide `docs/guides/texture-uploads.html`). Every level of a load is made on a worker (§6): `buildTextureLevels` (`src/rhi/mipChain.cpp`, `stb_image_resize2`, each level from the one above with its default Mitchell filter) packs level 0 and the mips into one `TextureRaw`: `R8G8B8A8_SRGB` in linear light, weighted by alpha; `R8G8B8A8_UNORM` as stored; renormalized (`renormalize`, each texel's xyz made unit length again after the resize) for a glTF normal texture. The count is `getFullMipCount`, the texture's. The main thread only copies: `RenderContext::fillTexture` takes every mip (`TextureLevel` per level; the one-pointer form, for an application's one RGBA8 level, still builds the chain in the calling thread with `buildMipChain`), and checks each level's size (`rhi::getTextureLevelSize`: whole 4 × 4 blocks for a block format):
  - where `VulkanDevice::queryFormatSupport` gave the format host copy, `VulkanDevice::fillTexture` transitions the image on the host and fills it with `vkCopyMemoryToImage`, so no command buffer or staging buffer is involved. The texture was created with `HOST_TRANSFER` usage (`rhi::Texture::hostCopy`). A format gets it only when the `hostImageCopy` feature is on (it is optional) and `EngineConfig::hostImageCopy` is true, its optimal-tiling features have `HOST_IMAGE_TRANSFER`, `VkHostImageCopyDevicePerformanceQuery::optimalDeviceAccess` is true, `SHADER_READ_ONLY_OPTIMAL` is among `pCopyDstLayouts`, and `getHostCopyMemoryTypes` (`vulkanUtils.cpp`) finds a device-local memory type that a 256 × 256 image of the format may use both with `HOST_TRANSFER` and with `TRANSFER_DST` (`vkGetDeviceImageMemoryRequirements`, `VulkanDevice::getImageMemoryTypes`). `FormatSupport::hostCopyTypes` keeps those types, and `createTexture` allocates the image only from them (`memoryTypeBits`); when that fails, it makes the image for the staging ring instead. The NVIDIA driver (RTX 3060, 610) lets `HOST_TRANSFER` images use only host-visible types (system RAM, or VRAM through resizable BAR), and while a model's textures were host-copied into the BAR type its GPU stalled for 50 to 185 ms, so there host copy is off; lavapipe has one memory type and copies 13 formats on the host;
  - otherwise the levels go through the staging ring (`UploadManager::uploadTexture`, §12). An application's fill reaches the image with the next submit, before any frame that samples it. A load's fill goes on the load uploads' queue in parts (`RenderContext::fillTexturePart`, §6), and the texture counts as loaded only once its last part is on the GPU. The texture was created with `TRANSFER_DST` usage instead.
- **Texture memory.** No image asks VMA for host access. Sampled textures are sub-allocated from VMA's blocks (256 MiB on a large heap), so creating and freeing one is rarely a `vkAllocateMemory` or `vkFreeMemory` (`P22`: in `17_streaming`, Release, `collectReleases` takes 0.020 ms per frame on average and at most 0.15 ms, against 0.61 and 1.42 ms with an allocation per texture, so it needs no time budget). Attachments and storage textures keep a dedicated allocation, and transient render targets are placed in heaps (§9). `VRAMStats::vramUsage` (the driver's heap usage) counts whole blocks; `vramAllocated` (VMA's `allocationBytes` in the device-local heaps) counts the resources in them. The stats overlay and the RASM window show both.
- **Block-compressed textures.** `Format` has `BC4_UNORM`, `BC5_UNORM`, `BC7_UNORM`/`_SRGB`, `ASTC_4x4_UNORM`/`_SRGB`, `ETC2_R8G8B8A8_UNORM`/`_SRGB`, `EAC_R11_UNORM` and `EAC_R11G11_UNORM`. `RenderContext::supportsTextureFormat` says which the device samples with linear filtering (`VulkanDevice::queryFormatSupport`, once at startup: the compression feature is on, and the format's optimal-tiling features), and `createTexture` refuses a `SAMPLED` texture in any other. Only KTX2 files use them today (§6); see the guide `docs/guides/texture-compression.html`.
- **Sampling.** `RenderContext::initialize` creates one sampler per `SamplerType`: linear or nearest, each with repeat, mirrored repeat or clamp (linear ones use 8× anisotropy; none clamps the LOD, so each serves any mip count). A bindless slot is written with the sampler passed to `addBindlessTexture`, `LINEAR_REPEAT` by default. glTF textures pick theirs from the glTF sampler (`wrapS` for both axes, `magFilter` for nearest or linear), and render target color attachments use `LINEAR_CLAMP`. A texture handle has one slot, so an image that two glTF samplers share is uploaded once per sampler.
- **Color spaces.** Base color and emissive are uploaded as `R8G8B8A8_SRGB`. Normal, metallic-roughness and occlusion maps are uploaded as `R8G8B8A8_UNORM`. A KTX2 texture follows its slot the same way: an sRGB format for a color slot, a UNORM one for a data slot, whatever the file says.
- **Two-channel normal maps.** A normal texture in `BC5_UNORM` or `EAC_R11G11_UNORM` holds only x and y. `writeMaterialRow` sets `MATERIAL_NORMAL_XY` in the material row from the texture's format (so `updateMaterial` keeps it right), and `getNormalFromMap` rebuilds z = √(1 − x² − y²) before the normal scale.
- Every material uses the uber shader, in the pipeline of its alpha mode (§4). To add a shading path, add a value to `gfx::MaterialId` and its mirror in `core.slang`, map it in `getGfxMaterialId` (`gpuScene.cpp`), and branch on it in `fragMain`. Today's mapping is `UNLIT` → `MaterialId::UNLIT` (base color only, no lights) and `PBR` → `MaterialId::PBR`.

## 6. Asset import

**Background loading (`P6`, `P21`, `P14`).** `loadMesh`, `loadGLTF`, `loadTexture` and `loadEnvironment` return a handle at once and load on the engine's worker pool (an environment is then baked on the GPU, §4). `finishMeshLoad` logs the time it took on the main thread. The guide is `docs/guides/job-system.html`.

```
 main thread                         WorkerPool (loadThreads workers)
 ───────────                         ─────────────────────────────────
 loadMesh(path) ── handle (LOADING) ─▶ load queue ─▶ runMeshImport: parseMeshFile
                                        │              (geometry, animations; images only planned)
                                        │            ├─ child: runImageWork per image (decode or transcode, mips)
                                        │            └─ child: buildMeshLods, then buildMeshlets, per sub-mesh
                                        │            the last child: finishImageWork, finishLoad
 loadTexture(path)                      ├─▶ decodeTexture / decodeKtxFile, finishLoad
   reads header, creates texture        └─▶ (a load starts only if its bytes fit loadMemoryBudget)
 beginFrame → finishLoads ◀── ticket DONE ── GLTFRaw / TextureRaw (every level)
   publishUploadedLoads: what the load uploads' fence passed → mesh READY, texture loaded, environment bakes
   beginLoadUploads
   meshes: materials, textures, geometry → pool (state still LOADING)
     (a glTF's textures go into pendingTextures with their levels)
   textures: fillTexturePart, 4 MiB at a time, the ones begun first
   CPU data handed to a worker (freeOnWorker), which frees it, then gives its bytes back to the budget
   stops after LOAD_BUDGET (4 ms) per frame
   endLoadUploads: the copies go out now (transfer queue, else graphics), value V for all of them
```

- **The loader** (`AssetLoader`, `docs/api/engine.html#AssetLoader`). `AssetLoader(Renderer&, AssetLoaderConfig)` attaches itself to the renderer (`Renderer::loads`, one loader per renderer; a second refuses every call), and the renderer's `beginFrame` calls its `finishLoads` through `LoadHooks`. An `Engine` owns one, made from its `EngineConfig` (`toAssetLoaderConfig`). The loader reads and writes the renderer's objects as its friend; the renderer keeps its own state of a load's result: a mesh's `MeshState`, and `Renderer::loadingTextures`, the textures a loader made that are not filled yet, which `isLoaded(texture)` and the entities that wait for textures read (`textureLoaded` takes one out when its copies are done). `destroyMesh`, `destroyTexture` and `destroyEnvironment` call `LoadHooks::forget`, which drops the object's load and its path from the caches. `waitForLoads` and `getLoadStats` on the renderer ask the attached loader. A loader on a renderer that did not start finishes nothing. A loader destroyed while its renderer lives waits for and publishes the copies in flight, then fails the meshes and environments still loading (`MeshState::FAILED`, `BakeState::FAILED`) and destroys the textures still loading; a refused loader (a second one) starts no workers.
- **The pool** (`src/core/workerPool.cpp`, tests in `tests/workerPoolTests.cpp`). The `AssetLoader` makes it first in its constructor: `AssetLoaderConfig::loadThreads` threads (0: the hardware threads minus one for the main thread, at least 1), named `RASM loader N` in Tracy, each below the main thread's priority (`lowerThreadPriority`: Windows `THREAD_PRIORITY_BELOW_NORMAL`, Linux nice + 5, macOS the default QoS class at relative priority -5), so a worker the main thread wakes takes a free core instead of the main thread's (`P34`). A job is a `std::function`. Workers take the child jobs that a running job pushed (`pushChild`) first, in order, then the loads (`push`, or `pushNext` at the front for a started load's next step) in order, so a started model finishes before new loads start; a priority would be a sort key in the load queue. No job waits for another: `spawnMeshChildJobs` sets `MeshLoadJob::remaining` to its child count, and the child that brings it to 0 runs `completeMeshLoad`, so a pool of one thread runs any load.
- **The budget.** The pool counts the bytes of started loads and of results not yet used (`AssetLoaderConfig::loadMemoryBudget`, 512 MiB). A load starts only when its estimate fits next to them, or when nothing is held; until then it waits in the queue as a path. Child jobs are not held back: their bytes are their load's. Estimates: a texture's levels from its header (and an image's decoded pixels while its job makes the mips); an environment's RGBA16F texels and the floats it is decoded from; a mesh's file size until `parseMeshFile` returns (a `.gltf`'s buffers and images are read during the parse, so the workers' parses are bounded by their count, not by the budget), then its geometry and its images' file bytes (`GltfImageWork::fileBytes`). Its decoded images are a next step (`pushNext`: first in the load queue, gated like a load, its bytes added to the ticket's) that spawns the child jobs once their levels (`GltfImageWork::bytes`, from the headers) fit; the bytes a load already holds never hold back its own next step. `setLoadBytes` sets the real size when the load ends. Once the main thread has used a result (`finishReadyLoads`), `AssetLoader::Impl::freeOnWorker` pushes its last reference as a child job: a worker frees it and only then gives its bytes back, so the budget does not count a result as gone while it is still in memory, and the main thread never frees a block of several MiB (0.15 to 0.3 ms each, which gives pages back to the OS: `P26`, `docs/guides/frame-allocations.html`); a stopping pool frees it on the caller. A glTF texture's levels take their share with them (`PendingTextureLoad::bytes`, counted once per levels in `GltfImportCache::movedBytes`, `PendingMeshLoad::movedBytes`) until that texture is filled. A model bigger than the budget still runs, alone. `Renderer::getLoadStats` returns the workers, the queued and running jobs, and the bytes held now and at most (`LoadStats`); the RASM window shows it on its Loads line.
- **Tickets and cancel.** Each load has a `LoadTicket` (`src/core/workerPool.h`): `cancelled`, a state (`RUNNING`, `DONE`, `DROPPED`) and the bytes it holds. A load's jobs write its `MeshLoadJob`, `TextureLoadJob` or `EnvironmentLoadJob` (`src/assets/loadJobs.h`), which they and the pending entry hold by `shared_ptr`, then `finishLoad` sets `DONE`. `destroyMesh`, `destroyTexture` and `destroyEnvironment` (through `LoadHooks::forget`) erase the pending entry and call `dropPendingLoad`, which calls `dropLoad` and frees a finished result on a worker like `freeOnWorker`: a queued load is skipped when a worker reaches it (a queued next step gives back what the earlier step held), a running one checks `cancelled` between steps, and the side that lets go of the result last gives its bytes back (the state changes by compare-exchange): the job when it ends `DROPPED`, or, for a load already `DONE`, the worker that frees the result, since `dropLoad` returns those bytes instead of giving them back. `~Engine` drops every load and joins the workers before anything else.
- **Waiting.** `finishLoads(false)` is one budgeted pass (`finishReadyLoads`). `waitForLoads` loops: finish everything ready (which gives bytes back), then wait for the load uploads of that pass (`RenderContext::waitForLoadUploads`) if they are not done, else `waitForCompletion` until another load ends; so a load queued behind the budget always gets to start, and everything is loaded when it returns.
- **Load uploads** (`D13`; the guide `docs/guides/transfer-queue.html`). `finishReadyLoads` makes its fills between `RenderContext::beginLoadUploads` and `endLoadUploads`. They go to the load uploads' `UploadManager`: on the transfer queue, or the frames' own on the graphics queue when there is none, and `endLoadUploads` submits them at once and returns the value they are done at. Nothing a load writes is in use: a new texture, or ranges of the geometry and animation pools that the release queue gave back once every queue was done with them; so the copies wait only for the frame and compute values the CPU saw reached (free, and the order the spec asks for). A mesh stays `LOADING` (`uploadingMeshes`), a texture stays in `pendingTextures` (`filled`, `uploadValue`) and an environment does not bake (`Environment::uploadValue`) until `publishUploadedLoads`, at the start of the next `finishReadyLoads`, sees the load uploads' fence (`updateLoadUploadsDone`, no wait) pass the value. The frames' submits then wait for that value, already reached, and the first graphics one acquires the textures it released (§12), so a frame never waits for copies in flight. A mesh whose geometry was written straight into mapped memory (resizable BAR, lavapipe) waits the same way, a frame or two.

- The worker never touches the engine. Everything that creates GPU objects or writes the registry runs on the main thread in `finishLoads`, at the start of `beginFrame`, before anything binds the bindless set or the geometry pool. The bindless set has `UPDATE_AFTER_BIND`, so new slots can be written while the previous frame is still on the GPU, and geometry uploads go out with the frame's submit (§12).
- `finishLoads` finishes meshes, then environments, then textures, and stops once it has spent `LOAD_BUDGET` (4 ms) in a frame; the load that crosses the budget still finishes, so loads that end together spread over several frames. It fills at least one part of a ready texture per frame, so meshes that use up the budget frame after frame do not hold the textures back. A texture is filled in parts of `TEXTURE_FILL_PART` (4 MiB of rows, about 0.6 ms of copying) with the budget checked between them (`PendingTextureLoad::progress`, `TextureFillProgress`), the textures already begun first, and a part stops where the staging ring is full instead of waiting for the GPU to free it (`P24`). An entity whose mesh is `READY` leaves `entitiesWaitingForMesh` and waits for its textures (§4), also while its own glTF's textures fill. A glTF's `finishMeshLoad` creates its textures and puts each in `pendingTextures` with its levels already made (`PendingTextureLoad::levels`, shared with the `GLTFRaw` maps), so a model's copies spread over frames too, after its geometry. One step is still never split: a model's geometry goes in one `finishMeshLoad` (`P24`). `waitForLoads` has no budget, and fills a glTF's textures whole in the same call. Measured with `examples/10_ktx2_textures --measure` (Release, headless, RTX 3060, 23 workers, `tools/load_bench.py`, median of 5, transfer queue on): the whole load takes 109 ms for the Flight Helmet KTX2, 190 ms for the PNG one and 201 ms for Sponza, and the longest frame meanwhile is about 5, 5 and 8 ms (Sponza's is `finishMeshLoad`; up to 16 ms while the workers fill every core, `P25`). `17_streaming --burst` asks for 300 images in one frame and destroys 75 of them a frame later: all in after 433 ms, at most 512 MiB decoded at once.
- **glTF images** (`meshImport.cpp`, `P14`). tinygltf keeps every image's file bytes (`loadImage`). `parseMeshFile` plans the work from the headers: `planKtxImages` picks each KTX2 image's format per kind of slot, then `planImageLevels` adds each PNG or JPEG image that a material reads in a slot without a `KHR_texture_basisu` transcode, once per kind of slot (`getImageLevelsKey`: color, data with occlusion, normal). Each image is one `GltfImageWork` with a `GltfImageTarget` per format or slot, whose empty `TextureRaw` is already in `GLTFRaw::ktxImages` or `imageLevels`. `runImageWork`, one child job per image, decodes the image once (`stbi_load_from_memory` to RGBA8, a 16-bit image to 8 bits with an info line, `D36`) and makes each target's levels, or transcodes it once per format; it reads only its image and writes only its targets, so the levels do not depend on the order. `finishImageWork`, on the last job, takes out targets that failed, gives a failed KTX2 slot back to its PNG or JPEG source (made there), and frees the file bytes. `importMeshFile` does the same on one thread, for the tests. `loadMaterial` takes the transcode or those levels; the texture cache key includes whether the levels are a normal map's.
- **KTX2 textures** (`src/assets/ktxImage.cpp`, libktx; the guide `docs/guides/texture-compression.html`). `loadTexture` on a `.ktx2` path reads only the header on the main thread (`readKtxInfo`: size, levels, channels from the data format descriptor, Basis or not; the file is opened UTF-8-safe by `openFileForReading`). `chooseKtxFormat` picks the GPU format from what the slot reads (`KtxSlot`: `loadTexture`'s `format` gives `COLOR` for `R8G8B8A8_SRGB` and `CHANNELS` for `R8G8B8A8_UNORM`) and the formats the device samples (`getSupportedTextureFormats`): color → `BC7_SRGB`, `ASTC_4x4_SRGB`, `ETC2_R8G8B8A8_SRGB`, `R8G8B8A8_SRGB`; data → the same in UNORM; a two-channel file in a `NORMAL` or `CHANNELS` slot (a normal map's x, y) → `BC5_UNORM`, `EAC_R11G11_UNORM`, `R8G8B8A8_UNORM`; a one-channel file in an `OCCLUSION` or `CHANNELS` slot → `BC4_UNORM`, `EAC_R11_UNORM`, `R8G8B8A8_UNORM`. Any other slot keeps an RGBA format, so a one-channel metallic-roughness file still reads (r, r, r). The texture is created with that format and the file's mip count (`getKtxMipCount`). The worker reads the file and runs `decodeKtx`: a Basis file (ETC1S, UASTC) is transcoded (`ktxTexture2_TranscodeBasis`); any other KTX2 file keeps its format (zstd inflated), which the device must sample, read as sRGB or UNORM by the slot. A two-channel file transcoded to RGBA8 gets z computed on the CPU. A Basis file without mips is transcoded to RGBA8 and its mips are made on the worker with `buildMipChain`, with a warning that names the file and the fix (`warnAboutKtxFormat`); so is a UASTC file whose two channels are R and G (the transcoder's BC5 and EAC RG11 read y from alpha). A non-Basis block file without mips keeps its one level, with a warning. A decode or upload that fails fills the texture with constant blocks (`makeBlankKtxLevels`): white, or a flat normal for a two-channel format. The vcpkg port is built without libktx's ETC software decoder and OpenGL upload (`cmake/triplets/`).
- `isLoaded(mesh)` is true once a mesh is `READY` and none of the textures its import made (`Mesh::ownedTextures`) waits in `pendingTextures`, and false while it is `LOADING` or after it `FAILED`. `isLoaded(texture)` is true once its pixels are on the GPU (a lookup in `pendingTextures`, which is keyed by handle and keeps a filled texture until its copies are done). `isLoaded(environment)` is true once its bake is recorded. `waitForLoads()` blocks until every pending load is finished.
- An entity waits for its mesh and its material's textures (§4), so nothing is drawn half-loaded. A finished load marks the draw lists for a rebuild.
- An error found before the worker starts (unsupported extension, missing mesh file, out of handles, unreadable texture header) returns an invalid handle and caches nothing. A mesh that fails later is `FAILED` and draws nothing; `loadMesh` on its path again retries the load on the same handle. A texture whose decode fails after its header was read stays white, because a material may already point at it. A glTF texture whose fill fails is destroyed instead (`Renderer::destroyTexture` takes it out of its materials), since white in a normal or metallic-roughness slot would be wrong where the slot's default is right. A texture destroyed while it loads drops its load (see tickets above).
- **What stays in memory.** After `finishLoads`, the CPU vertex and index arrays, the `tinygltf::Model` and the decoded pixels are freed. The worker already drops the glTF buffers once every accessor is read. A mesh keeps only its `SubMesh` list (a `MeshGeometry` range in the pool, a node transform, a material and its kept node, `animatedNode`), its clips' names and lengths (`Mesh::animations`) and its morph weights' names (`Mesh::morphTargetNames`); the animations themselves are in the animation pool (§4, node animation).
- **Mesh LODs** (`EngineConfig::meshLods`, on by default). The worker calls `buildMeshLods` (`src/assets/meshLod.cpp`) on each sub-mesh after import, and `createMesh` calls it on the main thread. A sub-mesh under 1024 triangles gets none. Otherwise identical vertices are merged first (`meshopt_generateVertexRemapMulti`, over the vertex and, when the mesh has them, its colors, its skin and each morph target's deltas: at most 13 targets, else the mesh gets no LODs), because simplification only collapses edges between triangles that share vertices and OBJ import gives every corner its own vertex. Then each LOD is simplified from the one before to about half its triangles (`meshopt_simplify` with absolute errors, `meshopt_SimplifySparse` so a coarse level's cost follows its own indices, and `meshopt_SimplifyLockBorder`), and its indices are reordered for the vertex cache (`meshopt_optimizeVertexCache`), until `MAX_MESH_LODS`, under 64 triangles, or a step that removes less than a tenth. A LOD's error is the sum of its steps' errors, so it bounds the distance from LOD 0 and never decreases. The LODs are index lists into the sub-mesh's own vertices (`ObjRaw::lods`), so they cost only indices: up to as many again as LOD 0, about +20% of the mesh's pool memory with 64-byte vertices. Open borders are locked because each sub-mesh picks its LOD on its own: two sub-meshes that share an edge (a glTF mesh split by material) still meet at any pair of LODs. `createMesh` rejects indices that are not whole triangles or point past the vertices, before the builder reads them.
- **Meshlets.** After the LODs, the same job cuts every LOD of a sub-mesh of 1024 triangles or more (not skinned or morphed) into meshlets and writes the LOD's indices in meshlet order (§4, meshlets).
- **Vertex colors.** A vertex is 64 bytes, one cache line, so an attribute that only some meshes have is a stream of its own: `ObjRaw::colors` holds one packed color per vertex or is empty. `packVertexColor(linear)` packs RGBA8 with R in the low byte, RGB sRGB-encoded so 8 bits keep the dark shades, and alpha linear. `GeometryPool::upload` puts a sub-mesh's colors in the color pool and its row's `colorOffset` names where they start (-1 without), so a mesh without colors costs nothing there. `createMesh(vertices, indices, colors)` takes them from the application (`colors` empty, or one per vertex), glTF import from `COLOR_0`. A skinned glTF mesh's joints and weights are the same kind of stream (`ObjRaw::skin`, the skin pool, `skinOffset`, below); morph targets will need one too.
- **Geometry pool.** `GeometryPool::allocate` takes a range of each stream the sub-mesh uses and a table row, from one `RangeAllocator` each (all or none), and `GeometryPool::write` fills them: `firstVertex`/`firstIndex` are where it starts, and the coarser LODs' indices follow LOD 0's (`MeshGeometry::lodIndexCount`; the LOD count is only in the table row). It also computes a bounding sphere (the box center, and the distance to the farthest vertex) and writes the sub-mesh's row of the geometry table (`geometryIndex`, §4). `destroyMesh` gives the ranges back through the release queue, after the frames that may draw them (`GeometryPool::free`). `finishMeshLoad` allocates every sub-mesh's ranges, and checks the animation pool's largest free range, before it creates anything, so a mesh that does not fit logs the pool usage and the largest free ranges and fails without using pool space, materials or bindless slots; a failure after that gives back what it made. `createMesh` uploads straight away and returns a `READY` mesh; it takes the handle after the upload, so a mesh that does not fit uses no index. The pool, its sub-mesh rows and the animation pool are the only limits on meshes: the registry is a `std::vector` with no count cap.
- `AssetLoader::loadMesh(path)` dispatches on the file extension and caches by resolved path in `registry.loadedMeshes`, so a relative and an absolute path to one file share a mesh.
  - **OBJ** (tinyobjloader) is de-indexed into one vertex per index (welded again by `buildMeshLods` when the mesh gets LODs). V is flipped per vertex, because OBJ puts the UV origin at the bottom-left.
  - **glTF/GLB** (tinygltf) goes through `processGLTF`, which walks the default scene's node tree from an identity root transform (glTF is right-handed Y-up, like the engine). The walk uses an explicit stack and imports each node once, skipping out-of-range references and any node reached a second time (a cycle, or a node with two parents, which glTF forbids). For each mesh node, `extractGLTFRaws` reads `POSITION`, `NORMAL`, `TANGENT`, `TEXCOORD_0..2`, `COLOR_0` (VEC3 or VEC4, float or normalized, packed with `packVertexColor`) and the indices through one validated accessor reader (`gltfAccessor.cpp`). The walk also keeps each node's parent and matrices, from which `importAnimations` keeps the nodes and clips that animate a mesh (§4, node animation). That reader handles strides, sparse accessors and quantized or normalized components, which is all `KHR_mesh_quantization` needs: a quantized position is undone by its node's transform, a quantized UV by its texture transform. A file whose `extensionsRequired` names one RASM does not support (only `KHR_materials_unlit`, `KHR_materials_emissive_strength`, `KHR_texture_transform`, `KHR_mesh_quantization` and `KHR_texture_basisu` are) fails to load and logs the names; an unsupported extension it only uses is logged at debug level and ignored. Strips and fans become triangle lists, primitives without indices get generated ones, and point and line primitives are skipped. Missing normals are computed as smooth normals, and in that case `TANGENT` is ignored, as the glTF spec requires. Each primitive keeps its glTF material index, and `finishMeshLoad` calls `loadMaterial` for it on the main thread.
- **Tangents and normal maps.** `Vertex::tangent` holds the glTF tangent with w = ±1, or w = 0 when the mesh has none. The vertex shader transforms it with the model matrix and multiplies w by the sign of the matrix's determinant, so mirroring transforms such as a negative node scale keep the bitangent right. `getNormalFromMap` uses the authored frame (B = cross(N, T)·w) when w ≠ 0. Otherwise it reconstructs a frame from UV derivatives, with B pointing toward decreasing v (glTF normal maps store +Y as up in the image).
- `loadMaterial` maps a glTF PBR material to `rasm::Material`: the base color with its alpha, `alphaMode` and `alphaCutoff`, each texture reference's `KHR_texture_transform` (whose `texCoord`, when present, replaces the reference's), and `KHR_materials_emissive_strength`. Materials with `KHR_materials_unlit` become `MaterialType::UNLIT`, and only their base color texture is uploaded. An image job decodes a PNG or JPEG to RGBA8 (see glTF images above). A KTX2 image is kept as it is (`loadImage`, the importer's tinygltf image loader), and the import picks a format for each (image, slot) that a material uses (`planKtxImages`: base color and emissive `COLOR`, metallic-roughness `DATA`, the normal map `NORMAL`, occlusion `OCCLUSION`; unlit materials only base color); each image is transcoded once per format by its own job, and its file bytes go when the last job of the model ends. `GLTFRaw::ktxFormats` maps `getKtxSlotKey(image, slot)` to the format and `GLTFRaw::ktxImages` maps `getKtxImageKey(image, format)` to the levels, so an ORM image read as occlusion and as metallic-roughness is one BC7 texture. `loadMaterial` uses a texture's `KHR_texture_basisu` source (`getBasisuSource`) when its transcode is there, and the texture's own `source` (a PNG or JPEG fallback) otherwise. `finishMeshLoad` creates a `GltfImportCache` for each import, pointing at the `GLTFRaw` with the transcoded images, and passes it to `loadMaterial`. It caches materials by material index and textures by image index, sampler and format, so primitives that share a material share one `MaterialHandle` and one set of bindless slots.
- A primitive without a material uses its entity's material. `getMaterial` resolves an invalid handle to `defaultMaterial`, the glTF default material (white, metallic 1, roughness 1) created at startup.

**Tile streaming** (`TileStreamer`, `include/rasm/tileStreamer.h`, `src/scene/tileStreamer.cpp`, `src/scene/tileResidency.cpp`; the guide `docs/guides/tile-streaming.html`). It keeps one scene's tiles in GPU memory under a byte budget, on top of the load and destroy paths above; it is in `rasm::renderer` and loads nothing itself.

```
 application (each frame)                     TileStreamer::update                              renderer
 ───────────────────────                      ────────────────────                              ────────
 setViews(views) ─────────────────────▶ TileView per view of its scene (planes, pixels per unit, doubles)
 walk its tree: getScreenError(tile)
 update(cut) ─────────────────────────▶ poll LOADING tiles: READY / FAILED / size so far ──────▶ isLoaded, MeshState, EntityDrawState
                                        limits: budget (memoryBudget, or deviceShare of what  ──▶ getPoolStats, queryMemoryStats
                                          the device budget leaves) and each pool's free rows
                                        TileResidency::update: wanted = cut + ancestors, ranks,
                                          drops (over a limit, unwanted oldest, wanted lower),
                                          hands out (maxLoadsPerFrame, maxLoadsInFlight), cancels
                                          unwanted loads, drawn set (stand-ins), per-update stats
                                        hide (removeEntityFromScene), destroy content, show ─────▶ scene membership, destroy*
 getTilesToLoad → make content → setTileContent(tile, {entities, meshes, materials, textures})
```

- **Split.** `TileResidency` is the policy on plain data (records by `TileHandle` index from a `HandlePool` of `maxTiles`, the held list, the wanted forest linked per update through `firstChild` / `nextSibling`), with no renderer, so `tests/tileResidencyTests.cpp` covers it. `TileStreamer::Impl`, a friend of `Renderer` like `AssetLoader::Impl`, measures content, polls readiness, reads the budget and the pools, and changes scene membership.
- **Cost** (`TileCost`, one number per `TileUnit`): bytes (textures: `RenderContext::getTextureMemoryBytes`, the VMA allocation's size kept in `rhi::Texture::memoryBytes`; meshes: vertices, colors, skins, morph deltas, indices with LODs, meshlets and meshlet data in the geometry pools; a glTF mesh's `ownedTextures` and `ownedMaterials` count with it), vertices, indices, sub-meshes, meshlets, instance rows (each entity's mesh's sub-meshes), materials and bindless slots. A tile holds its measured cost when `READY`, else what it reserved: its last measured cost, else the mean of every measured tile's with `TileDesc::estimatedBytes` for the bytes, raised to what its content holds so far while it loads (`updateLoading`, which also sets its real holdings, `TileRecord::real`). A tile dropped while it loads keeps that as its estimate.
- **Limits** (`Impl::getLimits`): bytes `memoryBudget`, or every 30 updates `deviceShare` × (`vramBudget` − (`vramUsage` − the bytes the tiles' content holds)); each pool its capacity (`RendererConfig` and `getBindlessTextureCapacity`) minus what anything but the tiles' content holds: `getPoolStats` (bindless slots: the capacity less `getFreeBindlessTextureSlots`, so slots that wait for the GPU count) less `TileResidency::getReal`, the tiles' real holdings, since a reservation of a tile still loading is in no pool yet.
- **Order.** Over a limit: first a tile that alone is over one, then the victims in order (unwanted by `lastWanted`, oldest first; wanted by rank, lowest and deepest first), within `maxDropsPerFrame`. Candidates (wanted `EMPTY` tiles, and `FAILED` ones after `retryFailedAfter`) by rank, highest first; one that cannot fit even with every lower victim dropped (`canFree`) waits and the next tries, up to 16 misses. A full `maxLoadsInFlight` cancels the longest-unwanted tile in flight. Rank: the screen error of max(own error, parent's error) at the tile's sphere, raised to its wanted children's.
- **Drawn set.** `cover` (bottom up: ready, or REPLACE with every wanted child covered) and `draw` (top down: a leaf if ready; an ADD tile if ready, and its children; a REPLACE tile in place of its children when one is not covered and it is ready, else its children). The update hides first, destroys the dropped content (entities first), then shows, so a tile and its stand-in swap in one frame. Entities join the scene only while drawn; `Renderer::rebuildDrawList` does the rest.
- **Ready** means the renderer's state: every content mesh `isLoaded` (`READY`, its import's textures out of `loadingTextures`), every texture `isLoaded`, every entity with `instanceCount > 0` and not `waitingForTextures` (so it draws as soon as it joins the scene). A content mesh `FAILED`, a handle the application destroyed, or an entity whose mesh is in but that has no instance rows for more than `ROWLESS_UPDATES` (4) updates (`syncGpuScene` gives up on it when the instance table is full) fails the tile and destroys the rest. `setTileContent` takes content only for a `REQUESTED` tile; content for a tile cancelled meanwhile is destroyed.
- **Allocations.** Every list keeps its capacity, and the C layer's conversion lists too, so an update with no tile change allocates nothing (the image test `tile_streaming`). Content changes allocate (handles' lists, the engine's creates and destroys).

## 7. Shader compilation

- `ShaderCompiler` has a `SlangContext` (a Slang global session and a session) for `compileFromString`'s thread, and one per shader worker (`WorkerPool` "RASM shaders", `RendererConfig::shaderThreads` threads). Slang's global session is not thread-safe and its objects are used by one thread at a time (`slang.h`), so no two threads share a context. A worker makes its context on its first cache miss (about 40 to 85 ms and 100 MB), and `endPrefetch` frees every worker's context once no prefetch is open: a warm run makes none, a cold one holds them for about 1 s (`Stats::workerSessions` counts them). A context keeps each module it loaded, named by the 128-bit hash of the source, so the stages of a pipeline and the entry points of a file parse their source once per context; the SPIR-V is the same as a lone compile's.
- `compileFromString(source, moduleName, entryPoint)` returns SPIR-V: from the shader cache, or from `compileWithSlang`, which loads a module, finds the entry point, composes, links, collects the struct layouts and gets the code. The session keeps every module and rejects a second one with the same name and different source, so the name it loads includes a hash of the source. Callers can reuse a name. A graphics pipeline compiles each stage (`P23`), on one module when one thread compiles both.
- **Warm-up (`P31`).** `Renderer::warmPipelines` (`src/renderer/pipelineWarmup.cpp`) queues `ShaderCompiler::Request`s (source, module name, entry point) with `ShaderCompiler::prefetch`, in the order the frames ask for them: the BRDF LUT, the tone map, culling, Hi-Z and light clusters; the shaded variants of `EngineConfig::antiAliasing` (`warmScenePipelines`: opaque and its mesh shader pipeline, sky, MASK, BLEND, grid, points, the point resolve, the MASK mesh pipeline, TAA or the MSAA resolve); shadows, meshlet culling, the environment bake, blend sort, Animate and Skin, Label Depth, bloom and the point raster; last the debug display tone maps, Inspect and the picks. `createSceneView` calls `warmScenePipelines(..., true)` for a view on a new kind of target (a second color: motion; several samples), which puts its shaders first. A variant's source is `getShaderVariant(defines, file)`, the same function the pipelines use, so both sides have one key; a shader asked under another key is compiled on the main thread, counted in the shutdown line and logged at debug level. A shader that failed is queued again the next time it is asked for (with `wait`, compiled at once on the main thread), and a context whose compile failed takes a new Slang session (`createSlangContext` keeps its global session), whose file reads are new, so a fix on disk, also of a `lib/` file, is picked up while the application runs. A worker's job (`runPrefetch`) takes the first queued shader and every other queued shader of its source, reads each from the disk cache or compiles it, and keeps the result in memory for the run (`prefetches`, about 50 KB a shader). `compileFromString` then takes a prefetched shader from memory, waits for it while a worker compiles it (Tracy `waitForShader`), or takes a queued one off the queue and compiles it itself. With `wait` false it returns `CompiledShader::pending` instead of waiting, and queues a shader no one asked for first (a disk cache hit is read at once): `createScenePipeline` and `createMeshScenePipeline` take a `pending` pointer for that, which only the debug views pass (§4, Debug views). Pipelines, `RenderContext`, the pipeline cache and the descriptor layouts stay on the main thread. `Renderer::waitForLoads` ends with `ShaderCompiler::waitForPrefetches`, so a run with `--wait-for-loads` (the image tests, `verify-change`) draws its first frame with every warmed pipeline ready. The mesh shader variants are warmed where the device can make them (`hasMeshShaderPipelines`), whatever the configured path, since `setMeshletPath` may pick them later. `ShaderCompiler::shutdown` drops the queued prefetches and joins the running ones before the renderer goes. Measured cold (`24_map_tiles --no-cache`, 8 threads): first frame 759 → 280 ms, longest later frame 451 → 84 ms, peak working set 510 → 1381 MB for about 1 s; warm: no change.
- Shaders are compiled **at runtime from source**, or read from the **shader cache** (`P8`, `docs/guides/shader-caching.html`), in `<cacheDir>/shaders/<key>.shader` (`readCachedShader`, `writeCachedShader`, `src/renderer/shaderCache.cpp`):
  - **Key.** A 128-bit FNV-1a `CacheKey` (`src/core/cacheFile.cpp`). `ShaderCompiler::initialize` makes the base key once: `SHADER_CACHE_KEY_VERSION`, the Slang build tag (`getBuildTagString`, the release, such as 2026.2) and the Slang library file's name, size and write time (`getModulePath` of `slang_createGlobalSession`, so a patched build of one release misses too), the target's format and profile, every entry of the target's and the session's option arrays, and every file under `shaders/lib` (relative path and bytes, `hashFolder`). Each compile adds the module name, the entry point and the source, which holds the variant's `#define` lines. A module that read a file outside `lib/` (`IModule::getDependencyFilePath`) is compiled but not cached.
  - **Entry.** `writeCacheFile`'s header (magic, `SHADER_CACHE_VERSION`, the full key, payload size, FNV-1a 64 checksum) and a payload: the SPIR-V, the push constants' struct name, and the `StructLayout` (size, members with the struct each holds, flattened fields) of every struct `pc` reaches, by its full name (`getFullName`: namespaces and generic arguments; a global struct's plain name), so two structs never share an entry. A wrong magic, size or checksum, or another version or key, is a miss: compile and write again. Writes go to a temporary file renamed over the entry.
  - **Check.** `validateRasmStructs` runs on those layouts for a compile and a hit alike (§4, CPU/GPU struct contract); `findStructLayout` reads them too.
  - **Size.** `pruneCacheFolder` at startup: past `MAX_SHADER_CACHE_BYTES` (256 MiB), the files written or read longest ago go until 3/4 remain; a hit sets the file's time.
  - `ShaderCompiler::shutdown` (from `~Renderer`) logs the hits, the compiles, their time and how many ran on the main thread. Tracy zones: `compileFromString`, `prefetch`, `runPrefetch`, `waitForShader`, `createSlangContext`, `compileWithSlang`, `loadModule`, `readCachedShader`, `warmPipelines`, and `VulkanPipelineCache::load` / `save`.
- **Pipeline cache** (`src/rhi/vulkan/vulkanPipelineCache.cpp`). `<cacheDir>/vulkan-<vendorID>-<deviceID>.pipelines`, one per GPU, holds the `VkPipelineCache` data behind a `writeCacheFile` header whose key is the vendor, device, driver version and `pipelineCacheUUID`. `load` also checks Vulkan's own header (`VkPipelineCacheHeaderVersionOne`) and starts empty on any difference, with one log line; every `vkCreate*Pipelines` gets the cache. `save` (in `~VulkanDevice`) writes only when the data's checksum changed, first merging a file another run saved since (`vkMergePipelineCaches`), and deletes the file past 256 MiB.
- `EngineConfig::cacheDir = ""` turns both off; the image tests and the unit tests do, and the examples take `--no-cache`. Release, headless, RTX 3060: `09_gltf_materials` starts in 2.43 s without caches and 1.02 s warm; its 24 pipelines take 1449 ms and 14 ms.
- The SPIR-V target is profile `spirv_1_4` with two capabilities (`getTargetDesc`): `spvImageGatherExtended`, which only silences a warning, and `spvDemoteToHelperInvocation`, which makes every `discard` an `OpDemoteToHelperInvocation` instead of an `OpKill`. A demoted lane stays in its 2x2 quad as a helper, so a cut-out's samples after its `discard` (the MASK `fragMain` reads the material's other textures) keep defined derivatives (`B33`). The device enables `shaderDemoteToHelperInvocation`, a core Vulkan 1.3 feature.
- The examples set `shaderDir` to the repo's `shaders/`, so a shader edit reaches an example the next time it starts, with no build.
- `tools/compile-shaders.bat` is an offline `slangc` invocation that is useful for checking compile errors. The engine does not consume its output.

## 8. Camera and input

- `Camera` holds a `CameraProjection` (a union of perspective and orthographic), a `CameraRig` (`rig`: a `CameraController`, trackball, free-fly or `NONE`, plus `easingSpeed`; the rendered `transform`; the `targetTransform`), an `exposure` that the tone-map pass multiplies the scene color by (§11; `setCameraExposure`, `getCameraExposure`), and a `Grid` its shaded views draw (§4, "Reference grid"; `setCameraGrid`, `getCameraGrid`).
- **Pan, zoom and the button map** (`docs/guides/camera-controllers.html#pan-zoom`). `CameraInput` has the orbit drag (`mouseStart`, `mouseEnd`), the pan drag (`panStart`, `panEnd`) and the `pointer`, all in the input's NDC. `CameraInputState::buttons` (`CameraButtons`) maps each mouse button, and Shift + left, to a `CameraDrag` (`NONE`, `ORBIT`, `PAN`; defaults: left orbits, right, middle and Shift + left pan, back and forward nothing); one drag at a time, ended only by its button's release; `pointerNdc` keeps the pointer. `Renderer::setCameraButtons` sets the engine's own. A trackball orbits with the orbit drag, moves `center` by the pan drag (at once, by the world units per NDC unit at the center: `radius * tan(fovY / 2) * min(1, aspect)`, or half the orthographic box's smaller side; 45° without a projection), and scales `radius` by `CAMERA_WHEEL_ZOOM` (0.9) a wheel step, between `MIN_TRACK_BALL_RADIUS` and `MAX_ZOOM_SIZE` (10^9); a pan or an orthographic zoom moves the rendered pose with the center, so the easing keeps the distance. `updateCameraRig(rig, projection, input)` takes the camera's projection: for an orthographic one the wheel scales the box (`left`, `right`, `bottom`, `top`) instead and moves `center` by the pointer's offset in the box times (1 − the scale) (`zoomOrthographic`), so the point under the pointer stays; the box's smaller half side stays in the same range. The box must have the view's aspect for the pointer to map right (the engine fits the width to the view; `D49`). `Renderer::updateCamera`, and so `beginFrame` and `rasm_update_camera`, pass the camera's projection. A free-fly camera looks with both drags. A trackball's `TrackBall::style` is `OrbitStyle::TURNTABLE` (the default: the drag's motion yaws around `TrackBall::up`, zero meaning +Y, and pitches, clamped to ±89° and rebuilt with `quatLookAt`, through `turnAroundUp`, so it never rolls and turns the same at the center and the edge; a step with no motion changes nothing) or `OrbitStyle::ARCBALL` (the two drag points on Holroyd's ball, `projectOnTrackBall`: a sphere for d² ≤ ½ and the hyperbola z = 1 / 2d beyond, so a drag past the rim goes on turning; a step that starts more than one unit from the center moves back to start there). A drag follows the motion: each `MOUSE_MOVE` adds `InputEvent::dx`, `dy` (else its change of position) to the drag's end, and a drag released within a frame gives its motion to `takeCameraInput` before it ends (a press before that starts nothing). `Renderer::setMouseGrab` calls `Platform::setMouseGrab`: the SDL platform's relative mouse mode hides the pointer and sends motion only, and puts the pointer back where the grab began; `beginFrame` grabs while the engine's own camera input drags (for a camera with a controller), and lets go only of the grab it took (`engineMouseGrab`). The SDL platform fills a button event's `modifiers` from `SDL_GetModState`, so Shift + left pans.
- **Controller helpers** (`docs/guides/camera-controllers.html`). The controllers are free functions on plain structs, with no `Engine`, so an application drives its own cameras with the same code: `createCameraRig(controller, transform)` (both poses at `transform`; a free-fly takes its yaw and pitch from the rotation) and `updateCameraRig(rig, input)` (the controller moves `targetTransform`, then the easing moves `transform`; `NONE` only eases; a free-fly controller with its own `FreeFly::up` turns its forward around it and rebuilds the rotation with `quatLookAt(forward, up)`, so up may change every frame; zero or +Y keeps the yaw and pitch angles of before; `setCameraTransform` makes the rig again at the new pose), in `include/rasm/cameraController.h` / `src/scene/cameraController.cpp`. `CameraInputState` turns `InputEvent`s into a `CameraInput` for one `InputArea` (a rectangle in window points): `addCameraInputEvent` (a press in the area starts a drag that lasts until the release, the wheel counts with the pointer in the area or before any event told where it is, a lost focus lets go of every key) and `takeCameraInput` (the frame's input without what the UI took, `UiInputCapture`), in `include/rasm/cameraInput.h` / `src/platform/cameraInput.cpp`. `getInputArea(window, rect)` (`rasm/view.h`) gives a view's `InputArea` from its `ViewRect` and the window's size in points (`examples/20_views`: one `CameraInputState` per view). The engine's path uses them: `setCameraController` is `createCameraRig` from the rendered pose, `updateCamera` is `updateCameraRig` on the camera's rig. An application turns the engine's controller off with `CameraController::Type::NONE`, keeps its own `CameraRig`, and sets the pose with `setCameraTransform` (`examples/05_city` on `getCameraInput`, `examples/19_embedding` with its own `CameraInputState` on `getInputEvents`).
- **Input events** (`docs/guides/platform-layer.html`). Every input is an `InputEvent` (`rasm/input.h`: a type and plain fields, for a later C API): `KEY` (`key`, the meaning in the layout; `physicalKey`, the position named after the US layout, `Key::NONE` meaning the same as `key`; `down`, `repeat`, `KEY_MOD_*` modifiers), `MOUSE_BUTTON`, `MOUSE_MOVE` (window points), `MOUSE_WHEEL`, `MOUSE_LEAVE`, `TEXT` (a codepoint), `FOCUS` and `WINDOW_CLOSE` (a window other than the main one was closed), each with the `window` it happened in (invalid: the main window). The SDL platform turns SDL's events into them, tagged by SDL's window id (`findWindow`) (`SDL_KEYS` maps keycodes and scancodes, UTF-8 text becomes one `TEXT` per codepoint) and captures the mouse while a button is held (`SDL_CaptureMouse`); the application of a `NATIVE` engine, or a script of a headless one, sends them with `Renderer::addInputEvent`. They queue in `Renderer::pendingInputEvents`. The first `pollEvents` after a `beginFrame` swaps that vector with `Renderer::inputEvents`, appends the platform's events and applies the main window's in order (another window's are only the application's, through `getInputEvents`): to Dear ImGui (`processUiEvent`) while it builds frames, and to `InputState` (`applyInputEvent`, `src/platform/input.cpp`) with the last UI frame's capture (`readUiInputCapture`, public as `getUiInputCapture`). Later calls in the same frame, and the one in `beginFrame` after the application's, leave the frame's input as it is: they only queue the platform's new events into `pendingInputEvents` and stop the engine on a window close, so a loop that renders no frames can still end. An event added after the poll waits for the next frame; `getInputEvents` returns the frame's list until the next poll. Both vectors keep their capacity (256 reserved, so a steady frame allocates nothing, `P27`). `InputState` holds the engine's `CameraInputState` for the whole main window (keys held by position, the pointer in points, the drag, the frame's wheel) and the frame's F-key and Escape presses. F1–F3 count by `key`, once per press without repeats, and not while a text field takes typing; Escape quits only with `PlatformType::SDL`. `setWindowSize` (points and pixels; 0 pixels skips frames as a minimized window does) and `setWindowContentScale` are the `NATIVE` and headless side of SDL's window events; with SDL they warn and do nothing. Unit tests: `tests/inputTests.cpp`.
- `pollEvents` measures `frameTime` (capped at 0.1 s) and sets `deltaTime`, the step the cameras, the UI and the stats' refresh use, to `EngineConfig::fixedDeltaTime` when it is above 0, else to `frameTime`. It applies the frame's `InputEvent`s (§8) and takes the frame's `CameraInput` (`takeCameraInput`, `getCameraInput`): mouse drag in NDC normalized by the smaller window dimension (`toInputNdc`), wheel delta, W/A/S/D held by position and the frame's `deltaTime` (`getDeltaTime`). `beginFrame` feeds that input to `updateCamera` for its camera, which updates the target with the active controller and then eases the rendered transform toward it with exponential smoothing. Within 1e-6 of the target (an absolute 1e-6 for the double position, so it holds far from the origin) it snaps to it, so a camera at rest does not move by rounding noise that depends on `deltaTime` (`B24`). The trackball eases along the orbit sphere.
- Window resize (SDL's pixel-size event, or `setWindowSize(window, ...)`) marks that window's swapchain for recreation; the main window's also updates every camera's aspect ratio. With the SDL platform, Escape or closing the main window sets `isRunning = false`, and closing another window is a `WINDOW_CLOSE` event for the application; a `NATIVE` or headless engine never stops by itself.
- `prepareSceneView` fits the camera's projection to the view's target with `getProjectionMatrix(camera, aspect)` and leaves the camera unchanged, so a render target of any shape renders undistorted. A perspective camera keeps its vertical field of view, and an orthographic one keeps its width.
- View matrix = `conjugate(rotation)` × `translate(−position)`, a `glm::dmat4` (`getViewMatrix`); the views draw with it moved into render space, where it is the rotation alone (§4, render space). The projection is reversed-Z: the near plane maps to depth 1 and the far plane to 0. A perspective `farZ` of 0 or infinity (0 is what an omitted `farZ` gives) means an infinite far plane, depth = `nearZ / distance`. Otherwise a perspective camera needs `0 < nearZ < farZ`, and an orthographic one needs a finite `farZ` greater than `nearZ` (it has no infinite form). `createCamera` returns an invalid handle and `setCameraProjection` keeps the old projection when these fail, with an error. `toProjectionMatrix` in `camera.cpp` gets the finite matrices from `glm::perspective`/`glm::ortho` (Vulkan [0, 1] range, `GLM_FORCE_DEPTH_ZERO_TO_ONE`, see `B2`) with near and far swapped, and writes the infinite one by hand.

## 9. Render graph (low-level path)

- `RenderGraph::addPass(name, setup, execute)` records the resources each pass reads and writes through `PassBuilder`. `compile()` builds edges between the passes and topologically sorts them (Kahn's algorithm, with cycle detection). The passes that write one resource (texture, buffer, render target or the swapchain) run in the order they were added. A reader runs after the last writer added before it and before the writer after that one, so a resource can be written twice in a frame, as a scene view's visible draws and depth are. A reader added before every writer reads the last one, unless it also writes the resource: then it reads what the resource held before this frame's writes. A depth read orders like a read of the target. `readPrevious(buffer)` reads what the previous frame wrote: it adds no edge from the writers and runs before this frame's first one. A cycle logs an error and empties the execution order, so `execute()` draws no passes and tries again next frame. It still runs the frame, which keeps the window handling events.
- `execute(camera)` runs `beginFrame(camera)` once, then for each pass in order records its barriers (below), then calls `setRenderTarget(pass.outputRenderTarget)`, `beginPass`, `pass.execute(ctx)` and `endPass`. A graphics pass declares where it draws, with `write(renderTarget)` or `writeSwapchain()`: exactly one of them, or `compile()` fails. Last comes `endFrame`.
- `addComputePass` adds a pass that `execute` runs outside any rendering scope: `pass.execute(ctx)`, with no attachments or clear. It sorts by its reads and writes like any other pass. Every pass gets a GPU timer named after it (§3, frame stats); compute passes next to each other with one `Pass::timerName` share one. It may write buffers, storage render targets and storage textures (below); a write of a target that is not `storage`, or of the swapchain, is dropped with an error, because it has no attachments. `addAsyncComputePass` drops a render target write too: a transient target's first pass must run on the graphics queue (Across queues, below). `examples/04_compute` animates particles in one and draws them in the next.
- **Storage render targets.** A target made with `RenderTargetDesc::storage` has colors, all `R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT` (the format shaders declare binding 1 with), and no depth (`checkRenderTargetDesc`; no cube). Its colors are `SAMPLED_STORAGE` textures, in the bindless set like any target's colors, and each color of each copy holds a slot of binding 1 for the target's life, both frame slots sharing one without history (`RenderTarget::storageSlots`, from `RenderContext::Impl::allocateStorageSlots` at `createRenderTarget` or `createTransientRenderTarget`: slots from `RESERVED_STORAGE_IMAGES` (32) up, all or none, with an error that names `MAX_BINDLESS_STORAGE_IMAGES` when they run out, so the target is not made). `createAttachments` writes each new texture into its slots, so a resize or a new placement keeps them; `destroyRenderTargets` gives them back (`releaseStorageSlots`), after its wait for the device. `Renderer::getRenderTargetStorageIndex(rt, attachment)` returns it. Only a compute pass writes one (`compile()` fails for a graphics pass that draws into it): before the pass, `execute` moves each color to `STORAGE` (from `STORAGE` it is a write-after-write barrier; the first pass of a transient target drops the contents, from `UNKNOWN`, since its memory held another target), and before any pass that reads a target (`read(rt)`) it moves each color to `SAMPLED`, which records nothing after a draw, whose `endPass` left it there. A compute pass's `write(TextureHandle)` moves the texture to `STORAGE` the same way; the texture must be `SAMPLED_STORAGE`. The bloom passes (§11) use 11 of them.
- `addAsyncComputePass` adds a compute pass that runs on the compute queue when the engine has one (`EngineConfig::asyncCompute` and a GPU with a compute family without graphics), and is a plain compute pass otherwise. The order is the same either way; only the batch it records into changes (§3).
- **Per-frame buffers.** `PassBuilder::read(perFrame)` and `write(perFrame)` take an array of `MAX_FRAMES_IN_FLIGHT` buffers, one per frame slot (`FrameBuffers` in the pass). Every slot orders the passes, but a barrier or wait covers only the slot of the frame being recorded, so a frame does not wait for the other slot's users.
- **Barriers come from the declared reads and writes** (`D9`). Before a pass begins, the graph records them, outside any rendering scope:

  ```
  pass declares         recorded before the pass begins
  read(texture)         transitionImageLayout(texture, SAMPLED)              nothing when it is SAMPLED already
  read(render target)   nothing: endPass of the pass that drew it left the color SAMPLED
  readPrevious(rt)      transitionImageLayout(previous slot's color, SAMPLED) first a clear, if no frame drew that copy yet
  readDepth(rt)         transitionImageLayout(the slot's depth, SAMPLED)     needs D32_SFLOAT, and occlusionCulling except for a depth-only or motion rt
  write(render target)  beginPass: attachments → COLOR / DEPTH               from UNKNOWN (contents dropped) when the pass clears them
  read(buffer)          memoryBarrier(last write's stage → this stage)       once per stage after each write
  readPrevious(buffer)  like read(buffer)                                    the last write is the previous frame's
  write(buffer)         memoryBarrier(last write + reads since → this stage) nothing for a buffer no pass used yet
  read/write(perFrame)  like read/write(buffer) of this frame slot's buffer
  ```

  For buffers the graph keeps an `Access` per buffer (`bufferAccess`): per queue, the stage of the last write, the stages that have read it since behind a barrier, and the value of that queue's batch that used it last. `bufferBarrier` folds all of a pass's buffers into one global `memoryBarrier` (§12), since shaders reach buffers through device addresses; a pass is `COMPUTE` or `GRAPHICS` as a whole. The state carries over between frames, so a compute pass that rewrites a buffer the previous frame drew from waits for those draws, and a draw at the start of a frame waits for a compute write at the end of the previous one. A pass that touches nothing another pass wrote or read gets no barrier. A pass must declare every buffer it uses, because an undeclared one gets no barrier at all. `compile()` fails, like on a cycle, for declarations that cannot be right: a compute pass with no write (of a buffer, a storage target or a texture, so one is missing), a pass that reads the render target it draws to, its color or its depth (it would be sampled while it is an attachment), a graphics pass that draws into a storage target, and a graphics pass that does not write exactly one render target or the swapchain. The graph tracks only its own passes, so a buffer two graphs share is not ordered between them. A pass with several dependent dispatches records `ctx.memoryBarrier(COMPUTE, COMPUTE)` between them.
- **Across queues.** A use on the other queue gets no barrier but a wait: before a pass, the graph calls `Renderer::waitForQueue(queue, other, value)` for each declared buffer the other queue wrote (for a read) or used (for a write), with the value of the batch that used it last. That closes the other queue's open batch if the value is its own, and makes this queue's batch wait for it at the start. The semaphore wait blocks all commands and makes the other queue's writes visible, so no barrier is needed. Textures (declared textures, render targets' attachments of this slot, the previous copy of a history target, the swapchain image and depth) are tracked the same way in `textureAccess`, but only across queues and with every use counted as a write, since the first use on a queue is often a layout transition. Before a transient target's first pass the graph also waits for the other queue's last pass this frame that used a transient target (`transientUses`), since it may have used the memory; the frame before is covered by `endFrame`'s wait, as a first pass draws, or writes a storage target in a compute pass that is not async, and so runs on the graphics queue. Undeclared resources get no wait, like they get no barrier. The state carries over between frames like the barriers', so a frame's compute pass waits for a previous frame's draw only through a resource they share. A graphics pass's `write(TextureHandle)` only orders passes. `examples/01_render_graph` and `examples/04_compute` use it.
- The scene's passes come from `Renderer::addScenePasses` (§4). Any other pass records through its own pipeline from `Renderer::createPipeline`. That example's full-screen passes (`Trail`, `Background`, `Present`) do the second: each binds a pipeline and draws a screen-covering triangle. A compute pass uses a pipeline from `Renderer::createComputePipeline`, which compiles an entry point of a Slang source string (`compMain` unless another is named) and uses the bindless set layout. `ShaderDesc::entryPoint` carries the entry point name to the pipeline, so the name is written only where the shader is compiled.
- **Load and store.** Each graphics pass carries `AttachmentOps` (`rasm/render/resource.h`): `loadColor` and `loadDepth` keep what the attachment holds instead of clearing it, `storeDepth` keeps depth at the end of the pass (color is always stored), and `firstWrite` says no earlier pass drew into the target this frame. `compile()` sets them in `assignAttachmentOps`: passes that draw to one target already run in the order they were added, so the first clears it, and each later one loads color and depth, which makes the one before store its depth. A depth read makes the last pass before it that drew the target store its depth too. Each face of a cube target counts as a target of its own for this (a pass draws face `f` with `PassBuilder::write(rt, f)`), so each face's first pass clears it; but the barrier before a pass covers all the cube's faces, so only the cube's first pass of the frame gets `firstWrite`. `execute` passes them to `beginPass` (with the face, through `Renderer::setRenderTarget(rt, face)`), which transitions an attachment it loads, or a cube depth without `firstWrite` (so a cube drawn by hand, with default ops, keeps every face), without `discard`, so the barrier keeps the contents and orders the earlier writes before the load.
- **Transient render targets.** `RenderGraph::createRenderTarget` (or `PassBuilder::createRenderTarget` inside a setup) makes a render target without `history` transient: `RenderContext::createTransientRenderTarget` gives its handle at once, with no textures yet. After a successful `compile()`, `transientLifetimes` records for each one the first and last position in the execution order of a pass that reads, reads the depth of, or draws it (a target no pass declares spans the whole frame), and fails the compile when its first use is a read, since its memory holds another target's data until a pass draws it. The next `execute` hands the targets and lifetimes to `RenderContext::placeTransientRenderTargets` (again after a recompile only when the lifetimes changed, or when a target has no textures because an earlier placement failed; until a placement works the frames draw no passes). It asks the device for each color and depth texture's memory requirements, packs them with `rhi::packAliased` (`src/rhi/memoryAliasing.cpp`, tested in `tests/memoryAliasingTests.cpp`): biggest first, each at the lowest offset that no texture with an overlapping lifetime uses, lifetimes inclusive at both ends, one heap per set of memory types. It then creates the heaps (`rhi::Device::createHeap`) and the textures inside them (`createPlacedTexture`), and logs the size against the size without sharing (`examples/01_render_graph`: two targets with five textures, 25.3 MiB instead of 42.2 MiB at 1920 × 1080). A texture that takes over memory needs a global barrier: its first use is `beginPass`, whose transition from `UNKNOWN` is an image barrier and orders only writes to that image, not the writes of the target that used the bytes before. So `execute` records `RenderContext::aliasingBarrier` (attachment writes and fragment/compute reads → attachment reads and writes, a `VkMemoryBarrier2`) before the first pass of each transient target, which also covers the previous frame's last use. Placing a group again (a recompile) first waits for the device to be idle, then frees the old heaps. The graph owns its transient targets: `~RenderGraph` (and a move assignment over a graph) calls `RenderContext::destroyRenderTargets`, which waits for the device to be idle, destroys their textures and handles, and frees each group's heaps once no target of it is left; a `RenderGraph` is move-only. A history target has its own memory; one made by the graph (`RenderGraph::historyTargets`) is destroyed with it, like the transient ones (`RenderContext::destroyRenderTargets`), so a graph built again (the `ViewGraph` after a change of view) does not keep the old one. One made with `Renderer::createRenderTarget` lives until shutdown.
- **Size.** `RenderTargetDesc::swapchainScale` above 0 sizes a target from the swapchain extent times the scale (at least 1 × 1), and ignores `width` and `height`. `Renderer::createSwapchainResources` calls `RenderContext::resizeRenderTargets` with the device idle: it rebuilds every such target that is not transient, and places again each transient group that holds one. Rebuilt textures take the old ones' bindless slots and keep their `TextureHandle`s (`RenderContext::createAttachments`: `Impl::replaceTexture` puts the new `rhi::Texture` behind the old handle and gives the old one to the release queue), so a resize does not use up the set, and a material or a UI image that holds a target's texture keeps working; `RenderTarget::version` counts the times the textures were made, for readers of their contents (TAA). A target that is not transient, history or not, is cleared again (below). `Renderer::resizeRenderTarget(rt, width, height)` gives a fixed-size target (not `swapchainScale`, not a cube) a new size: the same size returns at once; otherwise, between frames only (`Renderer::recordingFrame`), it waits for the last values the graphics and compute fences were given (the frames in flight may read the slots, which are written again), not for the device, then `RenderContext::resizeRenderTarget` makes the textures again (`createAttachments`), or places a transient target's group again (`placeGroup`).
- A render target has up to `MAX_COLOR_ATTACHMENTS` (4) color textures, one per format in `RenderTargetDesc::colorFormats` up to the first `UNKNOWN` (`colorAttachmentCount`; `Format::UNKNOWN` is the enum's first value, so unset entries are `UNKNOWN`), and one depth texture, all shared by both frame slots, and a `clearColor` that every color starts from when a pass clears. `beginPass`, `endPass`, `beginRendering` and the history clear work on every color; `createRenderTarget` and `createTransientRenderTarget` reject a desc with a gap in `colorFormats`, neither a color nor a depth format, or no color with `history` (`checkRenderTargetDesc`), since the pipeline formats would not match a target with a gap, and history keeps only colors. A desc with colors and no depth is a color-only target, a view texture for the tone map: its passes draw with no depth attachment (`beginPass` binds none), and `createPipeline(name, source, renderTarget)` makes its pipelines without depth. A desc with `cube` is a depth-only, square target of fixed size whose depth is a cube texture (`TextureDesc::cube`: an image of `CUBE_FACES` (6) layers, created `CUBE_COMPATIBLE`, with a cube view that the bindless set and `getRenderTargetDepthTexture` sample and a 2D view per face that `beginRendering` draws into); its passes name a face (`compile()` rejects a face past the target's), it gets no Hi-Z, and barriers cover all its layers, like every texture's. A desc with no color is a depth-only target, such as a shadow map: its passes have no color attachment (`VulkanCommandBuffer::beginRendering` takes the render area from the depth), and `createPipeline(name, source, renderTarget)` gives a pipeline without colors for it (it rejects a handle that resolves to no target, which has no depth format); `getRenderTargetTexture(rt, i)` returns color `i`. A pipeline for such a target comes from `Renderer::createPipeline(name, source, renderTarget)`, which copies the target's color and depth formats into `PipelineDesc::colorAttachmentFormats`; every color gets the same blend state, and `bindPipeline` compares all the formats with the pass's. `addScenePasses` needs a target with one `SCENE_COLOR_FORMAT` color, or a depth-only one. `examples/01_render_graph`'s `Background` pass writes two colors (the trail and a coverage mask) that `Present` combines. One copy is enough because a frame's passes run before the next frame's on the one queue, and the next frame's first use transitions from the old state, which waits for this frame's reads. A render target created with `RenderTargetDesc::history` has one copy per frame slot instead: a pass draws this frame's copy and reads the previous frame's with `PassBuilder::readPrevious` and `Renderer::getPreviousRenderTargetTexture` (invalid for a target without history, and `readPrevious` then logs an error and ignores the read). That read adds no edge, so a pass may read the previous copy of the target it draws to, as an accumulation pass does. A new copy is undefined until a frame draws it, so `createAttachments` queues every copy it makes for a history target (`HistoryClear` in `pendingClears`), and the colors of any target that is not transient and not storage, which may be sampled before a pass draws it (a view texture), and `Renderer::beginFrame` records `RenderContext::clearNewHistory` before any pass: each copy is cleared to `clearColor` and left `SAMPLED`, so `getPreviousRenderTargetTexture` and a view texture are safe to sample in a graph or not. Recorded clears move to `recordedClears` until the frame is submitted; `discardRecording` puts them back in `pendingClears`, and destroying a copy drops its clear. `examples/01_render_graph` keeps a fading trail of the bunny this way. `beginPass` moves the current slot's attachments (or the swapchain image and the slot's depth texture) to `COLOR_ATTACHMENT` and `DEPTH_STENCIL_ATTACHMENT`, with `discard` for the ones the pass clears, and sizes the viewport from the render target. `endPass` moves a render target's color to `SHADER_READ_ONLY_OPTIMAL`, the layout its bindless descriptor promises (`B10`), so later passes, in a graph or not, read it with `getRenderTargetTexture` and `getBindlessTextureIndex`. `endFrame` moves the swapchain image to `PRESENT_SRC`, once per frame, so two passes that draw to the swapchain get a `COLOR → COLOR` barrier between them instead of a present layout in the middle.
